Not really, no. I mean, I think about normal stuff, you know? Like, I guess I think about my GED class a lot because the test is coming up and I'm just trying to make sure I'm ready. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he's always saying how we need to practice our fractions, but he writes so small on the whiteboard that half the class can't even read it. And the markers he uses are always dried out. Like, he'll try three different black markers and they all just squeak and leave these faint gray lines. \n\nIt’s really annoying because I sit near the back, right by the window, and the glare makes it even worse. Plus, the window doesn't even shut all the way, so there's this constant draft, and yesterday it was blowing all these dead leaves right onto the sill. The janitor is supposed to fix it, but he’s always busy buffing the hallway floors. He uses this giant floor buffer that smells like strong lemons and it echoes so loud down the corridor. But yeah, no, I don't really have any thoughts that get stuck in my head or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in any physical activities outside of your work at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Um, physical activities outside of work? Not really, no. I mean, I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, so by the time I get home, I'm pretty wiped out. I usually just take the bus back, which is a whole thing in itself. The Route 42 bus is always late, like, without fail. Yesterday I was standing at the stop for forty-five minutes in the freezing cold. And the bus shelter there is completely useless because someone smashed the side glass last month, and the city still hasn't fixed it. There's just yellow caution tape flapping around in the wind. \n\nAnd then when the bus finally did show up, the heater was broken, so everyone was just sitting there shivering. The driver was this new guy who kept hitting the brakes way too hard at every single stoplight. I almost spilled my coffee right on this lady sitting next to me who was carrying, like, five giant bags of groceries. One of her bags had a bunch of oranges that spilled out and rolled all the way down the aisle to the front. We were all just watching these oranges roll around every time the bus stopped. It was ridiculous. But yeah, no, I don't really do any extra working out or anything like that. Just the warehouse and the bus ride, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you're eating so little?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess so. It's just, if I eat something, like, say, fried food from the break room or if I have a big plate at church, I get so scared it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. So I’ll just keep thinking about it non-stop and all I want to do is go home and check the scale. \n\nBut honestly, the break room at the warehouse is getting so gross anyway. They have this one microwave for all the packers, and someone exploded a Hot Pocket in there last Tuesday and just left it. The cheese is all crusted onto the glass spinny plate and it smells like burnt pepperoni every single time someone tries to heat up their lunch. My supervisor, Brenda, she put up this super passive-aggressive yellow sticky note on the door saying \"Your mother doesn't work here, clean up after yourself,\" but she spelled \"mother\" wrong. She wrote \"m-o-t-h-r\" and someone took a red Sharpie and drew a little arrow with an \"e\". \n\nIt's been a whole huge drama all week. Brenda was stomping around the packing line trying to figure out who corrected her spelling, like it's a major crime or something. She gets so mad about the littlest things, especially when we're trying to meet our quota before the freight trucks leave at four. The drivers always honk if we're even two minutes late bringing the pallets out. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just worry a lot that the food is going to show on me, so I try to avoid it and check my weight instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain when you decide how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I do think about my shape a lot. Like, when I look in the mirror before my GED class or before softball—well, I don't really play softball anymore, but I used to be in this church league. Anyway, when I look in the mirror, my stomach and my thighs still look bigger than they should. And honestly, how they look basically decides if I feel like I'm doing good or if I'm just totally failing for the whole day. \n\nBut speaking of softball, our team was called the Holy Hitters, which is kind of a dumb name, but Pastor Dave came up with it. He's always trying to be funny, but his jokes are just terrible. Like, he'll stand at the pulpit on Sunday and tell these long stories about his golden retriever, Buster, who apparently eats everything. Last week he said Buster ate a whole tube of toothpaste, the mint kind, and then threw it up all over his wife's prized petunias. \n\nHis wife, Mrs. Higgins, takes those petunias so seriously. She has this huge garden behind the parsonage, and she puts these little wire fences around everything to keep the rabbits out. But the rabbits just dig under them anyway. I saw one the other day just sitting right in the middle of her prize-winning tomatoes, munching away like it owned the place. It was a really fat rabbit, too, like it had been eating tomatoes all summer. But yeah, if my stomach and thighs look big in the mirror, it just ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Um, different from how others see me? I don't know, maybe. I mean, like I said before, when I'm looking in the mirror, especially before I head out to my GED class, I just... I look at my stomach and my thighs and they just look so much bigger than they should be. And it's like, if they look huge to me, it completely ruins my day. I just feel like I'm failing. \n\nBut my GED class, honestly, it's getting so hard to even focus there anyway. We're doing this algebra unit right now, and Mr. Henderson, the teacher, he talks so fast. He just writes all these equations on the whiteboard and expects us to keep up. And his markers are always running out of ink, so he's constantly shaking them or throwing them in the trash can by the door. But he always misses. There's like a pile of dead dry-erase markers just sitting on the floor in the corner of the room. \n\nAnd the guy who sits next to me, Kyle, he never brings a pencil. Every single Tuesday and Thursday, he leans over and asks to borrow one of mine. And he chews on the ends! I bought this pack of nice Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the good erasers, and he just gnaws on the metal part until it's all dented. I finally had to start bringing two separate pencil cases—one for me, and a decoy one with cheap plastic pens for Kyle. It's exhausting having to plan around someone else's weird habits, you know? But yeah, I don't really know what other people see when they look at me. I just know what I see in the mirror, and it's always my stomach and thighs looking too big, and it just messes with my head for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about how it would affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I didn't worry... maybe? But honestly, I'm only even here because the clinic got on me about how little I've been eating. Like, most days I'm just grabbing a coffee before my shift at the warehouse. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee at the warehouse is terrible anyway. They buy those giant plastic tubs of cheap grounds, the ones that look like buckets, and the water from the break room sink tastes like literal pennies. My coworker, Luis, he tried to bring in his own fancy French press thing once so he wouldn't have to drink it, but he accidentally knocked it off the packing table and glass went absolutely everywhere. We had to shut down line three for like forty-five minutes just to sweep it all up, and Brenda—remember Brenda? She was so mad she made him wear these giant plastic safety goggles for the rest of the week as a punishment. It was hilarious, but also kind of mean, because they kept fogging up while he was trying to tape the boxes and he couldn't see what he was doing. \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, eating more. Yeah, so I usually just grab that coffee before work, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. Because if I actually sit down for a real, full meal, it just feels like it completely throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't think about how your body would look afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... if I eat something heavy, like the fried food from the break room, or if I get a big plate of food at one of the church potlucks, I just get so scared that it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. I'll just keep thinking about it and obsessing, and I’ll want to go find a bathroom and check the scale right then and there. \n\nBut those church potlucks, honestly, they are a whole situation anyway. Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole, but she puts water chestnuts in it, which is just... it's a terrible texture. Who puts crunchy water chestnuts in a soft casserole? And Pastor Dave always eats like three helpings of it. I really don't know how he does it without spilling all over his ties. He wears the most ridiculous ties. He has this one with little neon golf clubs all over it, and another one that actually plays a song if you press a button hidden on the back. He wore the musical one to a Sunday service once, and I guess he leaned against the pulpit too hard because it just started blaring \"Jingle Bells\" right in the middle of his sermon. In April! It was so loud, and he couldn't figure out how to turn it off, so he just had to take the whole tie off and shove it in his pocket. He's always doing stuff like that. \n\nBut yeah, if I eat a big plate of that kind of food, I just panic about it changing my body and have to weigh myself immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably. Like, even if I didn't have a mirror, I'd still feel it, you know? I can feel how my clothes fit, or how my thighs touch when I walk. \n\nLike when I have to wear those heavy canvas work pants at the warehouse. They are the most uncomfortable pants in the world, I swear. The fabric is so stiff, it's like wearing cardboard, and the seams rub right against the inside of your knees. Just the warehouse and the bus ride, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you're eating so little?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess so. It's just, if I eat something, like, say, fried food from the break room or if I have a big plate at church, I get so scared it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. So I’ll just keep thinking about it non-stop and all I want to do is go home and check the scale. \n\nBut honestly, the break room at the warehouse is getting so gross anyway. They have this one microwave for all the packers, and someone exploded a Hot Pocket in there last Tuesday and just left it. The cheese is all crusted onto the glass spinny plate and it smells like burnt pepperoni every single time someone tries to heat up their lunch. My supervisor, Brenda, she put up this super passive-aggressive yellow sticky note on the door saying \"Your mother doesn't work here, clean up after yourself,\" but she spelled \"mother\" wrong. She wrote \"m-o-t-h-r\" and someone took a red Sharpie and drew a little arrow with an \"e\". \n\nIt's been a whole huge drama all week. Brenda was stomping around the packing line trying to figure out who corrected her spelling, like it's a major crime or something. She gets so mad about the littlest things, especially when we're trying to meet our quota before the freight trucks leave at four. The drivers always honk if we're even two minutes late bringing the pallets out. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just worry a lot that the food is going to show on me, so I try to avoid it and check my weight instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain when you decide how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I do think about my shape a lot. Like, when I look in the mirror before my GED class or before softball—well, I don't really play softball anymore, but I used to be in this church league. Anyway, when I look in the mirror, my stomach and my thighs still look bigger than they should. And honestly, how they look basically decides if I feel like I'm doing good or if I'm just totally failing for the whole day. \n\nBut speaking of softball, our team was called the Holy Hitters, which is kind of a dumb name, but Pastor Dave came up with it. He's always trying to be funny, but his jokes are just terrible. Like, he'll stand at the pulpit on Sunday and tell these long stories about his golden retriever, Buster, who apparently eats everything. Last week he said Buster ate a whole tube of toothpaste, the mint kind, and then threw it up all over his wife's prized petunias. \n\nHis wife, Mrs. Higgins, takes those petunias so seriously. She has this huge garden behind the parsonage, and she puts these little wire fences around everything to keep the rabbits out. But the rabbits just dig under them anyway. I saw one the other day just sitting right in the middle of her prize-winning tomatoes, munching away like it owned the place. It was a really fat rabbit, too, like it had been eating tomatoes all summer. But yeah, if my stomach and thighs look big in the mirror, it just ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Um, different from how others see me? I don't know, maybe. I mean, like I said before, when I'm looking in the mirror, especially before I head out to my GED class, I just... I look at my stomach and my thighs and they just look so much bigger than they should be. And it's like, if they look huge to me, it completely ruins my day. I just feel like I'm failing. \n\nBut my GED class, honestly, it's getting so hard to even focus there anyway. We're doing this algebra unit right now, and Mr. Henderson, the teacher, he talks so fast. He just writes all these equations on the whiteboard and expects us to keep up. And his markers are always running out of ink, so he's constantly shaking them or throwing them in the trash can by the door. But he always misses. There's like a pile of dead dry-erase markers just sitting on the floor in the corner of the room. \n\nAnd the guy who sits next to me, Kyle, he never brings a pencil. Every single Tuesday and Thursday, he leans over and asks to borrow one of mine. And he chews on the ends! I bought this pack of nice Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the good erasers, and he just gnaws on the metal part until it's all dented. I finally had to start bringing two separate pencil cases—one for me, and a decoy one with cheap plastic pens for Kyle. It's exhausting having to plan around someone else's weird habits, you know? But yeah, I don't really know what other people see when they look at me. I just know what I see in the mirror, and it's always my stomach and thighs looking too big, and it just messes with my head for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about how it would affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I didn't worry... maybe? But honestly, I'm only even here because the clinic got on me about how little I've been eating. Like, most days I'm just grabbing a coffee before my shift at the warehouse. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee at the warehouse is terrible anyway. They buy those giant plastic tubs of cheap grounds, the ones that look like buckets, and the water from the break room sink tastes like literal pennies. My coworker, Luis, he tried to bring in his own fancy French press thing once so he wouldn't have to drink it, but he accidentally knocked it off the packing table and glass went absolutely everywhere. We had to shut down line three for like forty-five minutes just to sweep it all up, and Brenda—remember Brenda? She was so mad she made him wear these giant plastic safety goggles for the rest of the week as a punishment. It was hilarious, but also kind of mean, because they kept fogging up while he was trying to tape the boxes and he couldn't see what he was doing. \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, eating more. Yeah, so I usually just grab that coffee before work, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. Because if I actually sit down for a real, full meal, it just feels like it completely throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't think about how your body would look afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... if I eat something heavy, like the fried food from the break room, or if I get a big plate of food at one of the church potlucks, I just get so scared that it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. I'll just keep thinking about it and obsessing, and I’ll want to go find a bathroom and check the scale right then and there. \n\nBut those church potlucks, honestly, they are a whole situation anyway. Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole, but she puts water chestnuts in it, which is just... it's a terrible texture. Who puts crunchy water chestnuts in a soft casserole? And Pastor Dave always eats like three helpings of it. I really don't know how he does it without spilling all over his ties. He wears the most ridiculous ties. He has this one with little neon golf clubs all over it, and another one that actually plays a song if you press a button hidden on the back. He wore the musical one to a Sunday service once, and I guess he leaned against the pulpit too hard because it just started blaring \"Jingle Bells\" right in the middle of his sermon. In April! It was so loud, and he couldn't figure out how to turn it off, so he just had to take the whole tie off and shove it in his pocket. He's always doing stuff like that. \n\nBut yeah, if I eat a big plate of that kind of food, I just panic about it changing my body and have to weigh myself immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably. Like, even if I didn't have a mirror, I'd still feel it, you know? I can feel how my clothes fit, or how my thighs touch when I walk. \n\nLike when I have to wear those heavy canvas work pants at the warehouse. They are the most uncomfortable pants in the world, I swear. The fabric is so stiff, it's like wearing cardboard, and the seams rub right against the inside of your knees. We have to wear them for safety because of the box cutters, but Luis—he's the one who broke the French press—he tried to get away with wearing these black denim jeans instead because they looked similar. But Brenda caught him because he bent over to pick up a roll of packing tape and the jeans ripped right down the middle seam. It was so loud, you could hear it over the conveyor belts. He had to tie his flannel shirt around his waist for the rest of his shift, and Brenda made him stand at the end of the line where everyone could see him. She's ruthless like that. She actually has this little notebook where she writes down every time someone messes up the dress code. \n\nBut yeah, even without a mirror, I’d still be checking how things fit and worrying about it. The feeling doesn't just go away because I can't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your weight if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know? I've never really thought about it as losing control, exactly. I'm not really sure what would happen. \n\nBut speaking of losing control, you should have seen line two at the warehouse yesterday. Total chaos. Someone—I think it was the new guy, Marcus—he loaded this massive shipment of ceramic garden gnomes onto the wrong belt. And the belt was going way too fast. Why do people even buy garden gnomes, anyway? They are so incredibly creepy. My aunt Sheila used to have this whole row of them leading up to her front porch, and over the years the paint chipped off their faces, so they just looked like these little zombie men staring at you when you walked up the steps. It used to freak me out so bad when I'd go over there after school. \n\nAnyway, Marcus's box of gnomes completely busted open at the end of the belt because it hit the stopper too hard, and there were just... shattered little red hats and ceramic beards everywhere. Luis was trying to scoop them all into a trash can with a flattened piece of cardboard like a makeshift dustpan, and Brenda was literally screaming over the loudspeaker. It took them like two hours to clear the jam. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I don't really know about the weight thing. I just haven't really tried eating more to find out what would happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't know if it's about losing control over my whole life... I mean, I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. I'm not even sure what that would look like for me. 

But honestly, my life feels pretty out of control sometimes anyway, especially with my schedule at the warehouse. They keep switching my shifts around without telling me until the day before. Last week, I was supposed to be on the morning shift, right? So I set my alarm for 4:30 AM, which is just brutal. My alarm clock is this ancient digital thing that my mom gave me, and the snooze button is broken, so you have to actually unplug it from the wall to make it stop beeping. It's the most annoying sound, like a dying smoke detector. Anyway, I dragged myself out of bed, unplugged the stupid clock, drove all the way there in the dark, and when I swiped my badge, the machine flashed red. Turns out Brenda had moved me to the swing shift and just... forgot to put it in the system. So I had to drive all the way back home and try to go back to sleep, but by then the neighbor's dog was outside barking at the mail carrier. That dog barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street? Barking. A car drives by? Barking. 

So yeah, I don't really know if eating more would make me feel like I'm losing control of my life, because I'm not really sure I have that much control over it to begin with.

It really does. Like, sitting down for a real meal just... it feels like it throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line. That's why I was saying, the clinic doctors are all over me about how little I've been eating, but I have my routine. I grab my coffee before the warehouse, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. \n\nBut you know, finding the right yogurt lately has been a whole thing because they completely rearranged the grocery store down the street from my apartment. It used to be so easy to just run in and grab my stuff, but now they moved all the dairy to the other side next to the frozen aisles, which makes absolutely no sense. And their shopping carts are always broken—like, every single one has a wheel that just locks up or pulls hard to the left. I was trying to push one yesterday and it was making this awful screeching sound the whole time I was in the store. It's so embarrassing when everyone is looking at you just because of a bad cart wheel. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just can't do a full meal. If I eat a whole plate of food, it just ruins my control over everything else I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I do worry. A lot. Like, if I eat something heavy, like the fried food they sometimes leave in the warehouse break room, or if I have a big plate at church, I get so scared it’s going to show on me right away. I'll just keep thinking about it constantly and I'll want to go home and check the scale immediately. \n\nBut honestly, I try to avoid the break room anyway because it is just disgusting right now. The main vending machine has been out of order for three weeks, and Dave refuses to call the repair guy because he says we're wasting company time looking at snacks. And don't even get me started on the microwave. Someone blew up a pepperoni Hot Pocket in there on Tuesday and just left the crust stuck to the glass. It smells like burnt cheese in the whole packing area, which is just gross. \n\nAnd then at church, they always have these huge potlucks after service. Our church actually just got a new pastor, Pastor Miller, and he talks so softly you can barely hear him over the air conditioning. The AC unit rattles so loud in the back pews where I sit, so I usually miss half the sermon. But yeah, if I even touch a big plate of food there, I just panic about the weight and have to go weigh myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Um, rituals? No, not really. I mean, I have my routine, like I said, with the coffee and the yogurt, but I wouldn't call it a ritual. It's just what I do. Although, speaking of doing things over and over, my neighbor's dog has been barking non-stop for the last three days. It's this little terrier mix, and it just stands at the fence and yaps at literally nothing. I tried talking to the neighbor about it, but he just brushed me off. He’s always outside working on this old Chevy truck that he’s had on blocks in his driveway since I moved in. He revs the engine at the worst times, like right when I'm trying to focus on my algebra homework. It’s so loud it shakes my windows. But yeah, no, I don't really have any rituals or anything like that. I just try to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um, thoughts I can't stop? Not really, no. I mean, I think about normal stuff, you know? Like, I guess I think about my GED class a lot because the test is coming up and I'm just trying to make sure I'm ready. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he's always saying how we need to practice our fractions, but he writes so small on the whiteboard that half the class can't even read it. And the markers he uses are always dried out. Like, he'll try three different black markers and they all just squeak and leave these faint gray lines. \n\nIt’s really annoying because I sit near the back, right by the window, and the glare makes it even worse. Plus, the window doesn't even shut all the way, so there's this constant draft, and yesterday it was blowing all these dead leaves right onto the sill. The janitor is supposed to fix it, but he’s always busy buffing the hallway floors. He uses this giant floor buffer that smells like strong lemons and it echoes so loud down the corridor. But yeah, no, I don't really have any thoughts that get stuck in my head or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in any physical activities outside of your work at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Um, physical activities outside of work? Not really, no. I mean, I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, so by the time I get home, I'm pretty wiped out. I usually just take the bus back, which is a whole thing in itself. The Route 42 bus is always late, like, without fail. Yesterday I was standing at the stop for forty-five minutes in the freezing cold. And the bus shelter there is completely useless because someone smashed the side glass last month, and the city still hasn't fixed it. There's just yellow caution tape flapping around in the wind. \n\nAnd then when the bus finally did show up, the heater was broken, so everyone was just sitting there shivering. The driver was this new guy who kept hitting the brakes way too hard at every single stoplight. I almost spilled my coffee right on this lady sitting next to me who was carrying, like, five giant bags of groceries. One of her bags had a bunch of oranges that spilled out and rolled all the way down the aisle to the front. We were all just watching these oranges roll around every time the bus stopped. It was ridiculous. But yeah, no, I don't really do any extra working out or anything like that. Just the warehouse and the bus ride, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you're eating so little?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess so. It's just, if I eat something, like, say, fried food from the break room or if I have a big plate at church, I get so scared it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. So I’ll just keep thinking about it non-stop and all I want to do is go home and check the scale. \n\nBut honestly, the break room at the warehouse is getting so gross anyway. They have this one microwave for all the packers, and someone exploded a Hot Pocket in there last Tuesday and just left it. The cheese is all crusted onto the glass spinny plate and it smells like burnt pepperoni every single time someone tries to heat up their lunch. My supervisor, Brenda, she put up this super passive-aggressive yellow sticky note on the door saying \"Your mother doesn't work here, clean up after yourself,\" but she spelled \"mother\" wrong. She wrote \"m-o-t-h-r\" and someone took a red Sharpie and drew a little arrow with an \"e\". \n\nIt's been a whole huge drama all week. Brenda was stomping around the packing line trying to figure out who corrected her spelling, like it's a major crime or something. She gets so mad about the littlest things, especially when we're trying to meet our quota before the freight trucks leave at four. The drivers always honk if we're even two minutes late bringing the pallets out. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just worry a lot that the food is going to show on me, so I try to avoid it and check my weight instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain when you decide how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I do think about my shape a lot. Like, when I look in the mirror before my GED class or before softball—well, I don't really play softball anymore, but I used to be in this church league. Anyway, when I look in the mirror, my stomach and my thighs still look bigger than they should. And honestly, how they look basically decides if I feel like I'm doing good or if I'm just totally failing for the whole day. \n\nBut speaking of softball, our team was called the Holy Hitters, which is kind of a dumb name, but Pastor Dave came up with it. He's always trying to be funny, but his jokes are just terrible. Like, he'll stand at the pulpit on Sunday and tell these long stories about his golden retriever, Buster, who apparently eats everything. Last week he said Buster ate a whole tube of toothpaste, the mint kind, and then threw it up all over his wife's prized petunias. \n\nHis wife, Mrs. Higgins, takes those petunias so seriously. She has this huge garden behind the parsonage, and she puts these little wire fences around everything to keep the rabbits out. But the rabbits just dig under them anyway. I saw one the other day just sitting right in the middle of her prize-winning tomatoes, munching away like it owned the place. It was a really fat rabbit, too, like it had been eating tomatoes all summer. But yeah, if my stomach and thighs look big in the mirror, it just ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Um, different from how others see me? I don't know, maybe. I mean, like I said before, when I'm looking in the mirror, especially before I head out to my GED class, I just... I look at my stomach and my thighs and they just look so much bigger than they should be. And it's like, if they look huge to me, it completely ruins my day. I just feel like I'm failing. \n\nBut my GED class, honestly, it's getting so hard to even focus there anyway. We're doing this algebra unit right now, and Mr. Henderson, the teacher, he talks so fast. He just writes all these equations on the whiteboard and expects us to keep up. And his markers are always running out of ink, so he's constantly shaking them or throwing them in the trash can by the door. But he always misses. There's like a pile of dead dry-erase markers just sitting on the floor in the corner of the room. \n\nAnd the guy who sits next to me, Kyle, he never brings a pencil. Every single Tuesday and Thursday, he leans over and asks to borrow one of mine. And he chews on the ends! I bought this pack of nice Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the good erasers, and he just gnaws on the metal part until it's all dented. I finally had to start bringing two separate pencil cases—one for me, and a decoy one with cheap plastic pens for Kyle. It's exhausting having to plan around someone else's weird habits, you know? But yeah, I don't really know what other people see when they look at me. I just know what I see in the mirror, and it's always my stomach and thighs looking too big, and it just messes with my head for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about how it would affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I didn't worry... maybe? But honestly, I'm only even here because the clinic got on me about how little I've been eating. Like, most days I'm just grabbing a coffee before my shift at the warehouse. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee at the warehouse is terrible anyway. They buy those giant plastic tubs of cheap grounds, the ones that look like buckets, and the water from the break room sink tastes like literal pennies. My coworker, Luis, he tried to bring in his own fancy French press thing once so he wouldn't have to drink it, but he accidentally knocked it off the packing table and glass went absolutely everywhere. We had to shut down line three for like forty-five minutes just to sweep it all up, and Brenda—remember Brenda? She was so mad she made him wear these giant plastic safety goggles for the rest of the week as a punishment. It was hilarious, but also kind of mean, because they kept fogging up while he was trying to tape the boxes and he couldn't see what he was doing. \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, eating more. Yeah, so I usually just grab that coffee before work, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. Because if I actually sit down for a real, full meal, it just feels like it completely throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't think about how your body would look afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... if I eat something heavy, like the fried food from the break room, or if I get a big plate of food at one of the church potlucks, I just get so scared that it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. I'll just keep thinking about it and obsessing, and I’ll want to go find a bathroom and check the scale right then and there. \n\nBut those church potlucks, honestly, they are a whole situation anyway. Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole, but she puts water chestnuts in it, which is just... it's a terrible texture. Who puts crunchy water chestnuts in a soft casserole? And Pastor Dave always eats like three helpings of it. I really don't know how he does it without spilling all over his ties. He wears the most ridiculous ties. He has this one with little neon golf clubs all over it, and another one that actually plays a song if you press a button hidden on the back. He wore the musical one to a Sunday service once, and I guess he leaned against the pulpit too hard because it just started blaring \"Jingle Bells\" right in the middle of his sermon. In April! It was so loud, and he couldn't figure out how to turn it off, so he just had to take the whole tie off and shove it in his pocket. He's always doing stuff like that. \n\nBut yeah, if I eat a big plate of that kind of food, I just panic about it changing my body and have to weigh myself immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably. Like, even if I didn't have a mirror, I'd still feel it, you know? I can feel how my clothes fit, or how my thighs touch when I walk. \n\nLike when I have to wear those heavy canvas work pants at the warehouse. They are the most uncomfortable pants in the world, I swear. The fabric is so stiff, it's like wearing cardboard, and the seams rub right against the inside of your knees. We have to wear them for safety because of the box cutters, but Luis—he's the one who broke the French press—he tried to get away with wearing these black denim jeans instead because they looked similar. But Brenda caught him because he bent over to pick up a roll of packing tape and the jeans ripped right down the middle seam. It was so loud, you could hear it over the conveyor belts. He had to tie his flannel shirt around his waist for the rest of his shift, and Brenda made him stand at the end of the line where everyone could see him. She's ruthless like that. She actually has this little notebook where she writes down every time someone messes up the dress code. \n\nBut yeah, even without a mirror, I’d still be checking how things fit and worrying about it. The feeling doesn't just go away because I can't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your weight if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know? I've never really thought about it as losing control, exactly. I'm not really sure what would happen. \n\nBut speaking of losing control, you should have seen line two at the warehouse yesterday. Total chaos. Someone—I think it was the new guy, Marcus—he loaded this massive shipment of ceramic garden gnomes onto the wrong belt. And the belt was going way too fast. Why do people even buy garden gnomes, anyway? They are so incredibly creepy. My aunt Sheila used to have this whole row of them leading up to her front porch, and over the years the paint chipped off their faces, so they just looked like these little zombie men staring at you when you walked up the steps. It used to freak me out so bad when I'd go over there after school. \n\nAnyway, Marcus's box of gnomes completely busted open at the end of the belt because it hit the stopper too hard, and there were just... shattered little red hats and ceramic beards everywhere. Luis was trying to scoop them all into a trash can with a flattened piece of cardboard like a makeshift dustpan, and Brenda was literally screaming over the loudspeaker. It took them like two hours to clear the jam. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I don't really know about the weight thing. I just haven't really tried eating more to find out what would happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel like you're losing control over your life?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's about losing control over my whole life... I mean, I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. I'm not even sure what that would look like for me. \n\nBut honestly, my life feels pretty out of control sometimes anyway, especially with my schedule at the warehouse. They keep switching my shifts around without telling me until the day before. Last week, I was supposed to be on the morning shift, right? So I set my alarm for 4:30 AM, which is just brutal. My alarm clock is this ancient digital thing that my mom gave me, and the snooze button is broken, so you have to actually unplug it from the wall to make it stop beeping. It's the most annoying sound, like a dying smoke detector. Anyway, I dragged myself out of bed, unplugged the stupid clock, drove all the way there in the dark, and when I swiped my badge, the machine flashed red. Turns out Brenda had moved me to the swing shift and just... forgot to put it in the system. So I had to drive all the way back home and try to go back to sleep, but by then the neighbor's dog was outside barking at the mail carrier. That dog barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street? Barking. A car drives by? Barking. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know if eating more would make me feel like I'm losing control of my life, because I'm not really sure I have that much control over it to begin with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

We had to shut down line three for like forty-five minutes just to sweep it all up, and Brenda—remember Brenda? She was so mad she made him wear these giant plastic safety goggles for the rest of the week as a punishment. It was hilarious, but also kind of mean, because they kept fogging up while he was trying to tape the boxes and he couldn't see what he was doing. \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, eating more. Yeah, so I usually just grab that coffee before work, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. Because if I actually sit down for a real, full meal, it just feels like it completely throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't think about how your body would look afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... if I eat something heavy, like the fried food from the break room, or if I get a big plate of food at one of the church potlucks, I just get so scared that it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. I'll just keep thinking about it and obsessing, and I’ll want to go find a bathroom and check the scale right then and there. \n\nBut those church potlucks, honestly, they are a whole situation anyway. Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole, but she puts water chestnuts in it, which is just... it's a terrible texture. Who puts crunchy water chestnuts in a soft casserole? And Pastor Dave always eats like three helpings of it. I really don't know how he does it without spilling all over his ties. He wears the most ridiculous ties. He has this one with little neon golf clubs all over it, and another one that actually plays a song if you press a button hidden on the back. He wore the musical one to a Sunday service once, and I guess he leaned against the pulpit too hard because it just started blaring \"Jingle Bells\" right in the middle of his sermon. In April! It was so loud, and he couldn't figure out how to turn it off, so he just had to take the whole tie off and shove it in his pocket. He's always doing stuff like that. \n\nBut yeah, if I eat a big plate of that kind of food, I just panic about it changing my body and have to weigh myself immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably. Like, even if I didn't have a mirror, I'd still feel it, you know? I can feel how my clothes fit, or how my thighs touch when I walk. \n\nLike when I have to wear those heavy canvas work pants at the warehouse. They are the most uncomfortable pants in the world, I swear. The fabric is so stiff, it's like wearing cardboard, and the seams rub right against the inside of your knees. We have to wear them for safety because of the box cutters, but Luis—he's the one who broke the French press—he tried to get away with wearing these black denim jeans instead because they looked similar. But Brenda caught him because he bent over to pick up a roll of packing tape and the jeans ripped right down the middle seam. It was so loud, you could hear it over the conveyor belts. He had to tie his flannel shirt around his waist for the rest of his shift, and Brenda made him stand at the end of the line where everyone could see him. She's ruthless like that. She actually has this little notebook where she writes down every time someone messes up the dress code. \n\nBut yeah, even without a mirror, I’d still be checking how things fit and worrying about it. The feeling doesn't just go away because I can't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your weight if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know? I've never really thought about it as losing control, exactly. I'm not really sure what would happen. \n\nBut speaking of losing control, you should have seen line two at the warehouse yesterday. Total chaos. Someone—I think it was the new guy, Marcus—he loaded this massive shipment of ceramic garden gnomes onto the wrong belt. And the belt was going way too fast. Why do people even buy garden gnomes, anyway? They are so incredibly creepy. My aunt Sheila used to have this whole row of them leading up to her front porch, and over the years the paint chipped off their faces, so they just looked like these little zombie men staring at you when you walked up the steps. It used to freak me out so bad when I'd go over there after school. \n\nAnyway, Marcus's box of gnomes completely busted open at the end of the belt because it hit the stopper too hard, and there were just... shattered little red hats and ceramic beards everywhere. Luis was trying to scoop them all into a trash can with a flattened piece of cardboard like a makeshift dustpan, and Brenda was literally screaming over the loudspeaker. It took them like two hours to clear the jam. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I don't really know about the weight thing. I just haven't really tried eating more to find out what would happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel like you're losing control over your life?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's about losing control over my whole life... I mean, I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. I'm not even sure what that would look like for me. \n\nBut honestly, my life feels pretty out of control sometimes anyway, especially with my schedule at the warehouse. I feel like... didn't you just ask me something like that? I honestly don't have a good answer for you. I haven't tried eating a "normal amount," whatever that even means, so I really don't know if it would make me feel out of control or not. 

But speaking of normal food, you should see the stuff people eat in our breakroom at the warehouse. It is so disgusting. We only have this one microwave for like, fifty people on the floor, and the timer dial is literally held on with duct tape. On Tuesday, Luis actually brought in leftover tilapia. Fish! It smelled so horrendous that Marcus started gagging over by the water cooler, and Brenda had to prop the double doors open with these heavy wooden pallets just to air the room out. 

But she left the pallets sticking out into the main forklift lane, so Dave—he's the safety manager—he came down and completely lost his mind. Dave has this really high-pitched voice when he gets mad, almost like a squeaky toy? He was yelling at Brenda and waving his clipboard around, and the clip snapped, so all his safety checklists went flying all over the concrete floor. It took three of us to help him pick them up, and the papers got all covered in that black warehouse dust. He was so mad he made Luis take his fish out to the dumpster in the parking lot. 

So yeah, I really don't know the answer to your question. I just haven't tried it to see what would happen.

And their shopping carts are always broken—like, every single one has a wheel that just locks up or pulls hard to the left. I was trying to push one yesterday and it was making this awful screeching sound the whole time I was in the store. If I eat a whole plate of food, it just ruins my control over everything else I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I do worry. I'll just keep thinking about it constantly and I'll want to go home and check the scale immediately. \n\nBut honestly, I try to avoid the break room anyway because it is just disgusting right now. The main vending machine has been out of order for three weeks, and Dave refuses to call the repair guy because he says we're wasting company time looking at snacks. And don't even get me started on the microwave. Someone blew up a pepperoni Hot Pocket in there on Tuesday and just left the crust stuck to the glass. It smells like burnt cheese in the whole packing area, which is just gross. \n\nAnd then at church, they always have these huge potlucks after service. Our church actually just got a new pastor, Pastor Miller, and he talks so softly you can barely hear him over the air conditioning. The AC unit rattles so loud in the back pews where I sit, so I usually miss half the sermon. But yeah, if I even touch a big plate of food there, I just panic about the weight and have to go weigh myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Um, rituals? No, not really. I mean, I have my routine, like I said, with the coffee and the yogurt, but I wouldn't call it a ritual. It's just what I do. Although, speaking of doing things over and over, my neighbor's dog has been barking non-stop for the last three days. It's this little terrier mix, and it just stands at the fence and yaps at literally nothing. I tried talking to the neighbor about it, but he just brushed me off. He’s always outside working on this old Chevy truck that he’s had on blocks in his driveway since I moved in. He revs the engine at the worst times, like right when I'm trying to focus on my algebra homework. It’s so loud it shakes my windows. But yeah, no, I don't really have any rituals or anything like that. I just try to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um, thoughts I can't stop? But yeah, no, I don't really have any thoughts that get stuck in my head or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in any physical activities outside of your work at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Um, physical activities outside of work? Not really, no. I mean, I'm on my feet all day at the warehouse, so by the time I get home, I'm pretty wiped out. I usually just take the bus back, which is a whole thing in itself. The Route 42 bus is always late, like, without fail. Yesterday I was standing at the stop for forty-five minutes in the freezing cold. And the bus shelter there is completely useless because someone smashed the side glass last month, and the city still hasn't fixed it. There's just yellow caution tape flapping around in the wind. \n\nAnd then when the bus finally did show up, the heater was broken, so everyone was just sitting there shivering. The driver was this new guy who kept hitting the brakes way too hard at every single stoplight. I almost spilled my coffee right on this lady sitting next to me who was carrying, like, five giant bags of groceries. One of her bags had a bunch of oranges that spilled out and rolled all the way down the aisle to the front. We were all just watching these oranges roll around every time the bus stopped. It was ridiculous. But yeah, no, I don't really do any extra working out or anything like that. Just the warehouse and the bus ride, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you're eating so little?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess so. It's just, if I eat something, like, say, fried food from the break room or if I have a big plate at church, I get so scared it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. So I’ll just keep thinking about it non-stop and all I want to do is go home and check the scale. \n\nBut honestly, the break room at the warehouse is getting so gross anyway. They have this one microwave for all the packers, and someone exploded a Hot Pocket in there last Tuesday and just left it. The cheese is all crusted onto the glass spinny plate and it smells like burnt pepperoni every single time someone tries to heat up their lunch. My supervisor, Brenda, she put up this super passive-aggressive yellow sticky note on the door saying \"Your mother doesn't work here, clean up after yourself,\" but she spelled \"mother\" wrong. She wrote \"m-o-t-h-r\" and someone took a red Sharpie and drew a little arrow with an \"e\". \n\nIt's been a whole huge drama all week. Brenda was stomping around the packing line trying to figure out who corrected her spelling, like it's a major crime or something. She gets so mad about the littlest things, especially when we're trying to meet our quota before the freight trucks leave at four. The drivers always honk if we're even two minutes late bringing the pallets out. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just worry a lot that the food is going to show on me, so I try to avoid it and check my weight instead.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain when you decide how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I do think about my shape a lot. Like, when I look in the mirror before my GED class or before softball—well, I don't really play softball anymore, but I used to be in this church league. Anyway, when I look in the mirror, my stomach and my thighs still look bigger than they should. And honestly, how they look basically decides if I feel like I'm doing good or if I'm just totally failing for the whole day. \n\nBut speaking of softball, our team was called the Holy Hitters, which is kind of a dumb name, but Pastor Dave came up with it. He's always trying to be funny, but his jokes are just terrible. Like, he'll stand at the pulpit on Sunday and tell these long stories about his golden retriever, Buster, who apparently eats everything. Last week he said Buster ate a whole tube of toothpaste, the mint kind, and then threw it up all over his wife's prized petunias. \n\nHis wife, Mrs. Higgins, takes those petunias so seriously. She has this huge garden behind the parsonage, and she puts these little wire fences around everything to keep the rabbits out. But the rabbits just dig under them anyway. I saw one the other day just sitting right in the middle of her prize-winning tomatoes, munching away like it owned the place. It was a really fat rabbit, too, like it had been eating tomatoes all summer. But yeah, if my stomach and thighs look big in the mirror, it just ruins my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Um, different from how others see me? I don't know, maybe. I mean, like I said before, when I'm looking in the mirror, especially before I head out to my GED class, I just... I look at my stomach and my thighs and they just look so much bigger than they should be. And it's like, if they look huge to me, it completely ruins my day. I just feel like I'm failing. \n\nBut my GED class, honestly, it's getting so hard to even focus there anyway. We're doing this algebra unit right now, and Mr. Henderson, the teacher, he talks so fast. He just writes all these equations on the whiteboard and expects us to keep up. And his markers are always running out of ink, so he's constantly shaking them or throwing them in the trash can by the door. But he always misses. There's like a pile of dead dry-erase markers just sitting on the floor in the corner of the room. \n\nAnd the guy who sits next to me, Kyle, he never brings a pencil. Every single Tuesday and Thursday, he leans over and asks to borrow one of mine. And he chews on the ends! I bought this pack of nice Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the good erasers, and he just gnaws on the metal part until it's all dented. I finally had to start bringing two separate pencil cases—one for me, and a decoy one with cheap plastic pens for Kyle. It's exhausting having to plan around someone else's weird habits, you know? But yeah, I don't really know what other people see when they look at me. I just know what I see in the mirror, and it's always my stomach and thighs looking too big, and it just messes with my head for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to eat more if you didn't worry about how it would affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I didn't worry... maybe? But honestly, I'm only even here because the clinic got on me about how little I've been eating. Like, most days I'm just grabbing a coffee before my shift at the warehouse. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee at the warehouse is terrible anyway. They buy those giant plastic tubs of cheap grounds, the ones that look like buckets, and the water from the break room sink tastes like literal pennies. My coworker, Luis, he tried to bring in his own fancy French press thing once so he wouldn't have to drink it, but he accidentally knocked it off the packing table and glass went absolutely everywhere. We had to shut down line three for like forty-five minutes just to sweep it all up, and Brenda—remember Brenda? She was so mad she made him wear these giant plastic safety goggles for the rest of the week as a punishment. It was hilarious, but also kind of mean, because they kept fogging up while he was trying to tape the boxes and he couldn't see what he was doing. \n\nBut, um... what were we talking about? Oh, eating more. Yeah, so I usually just grab that coffee before work, and then maybe I'll have a yogurt or just a few bites of something after my GED class. Because if I actually sit down for a real, full meal, it just feels like it completely throws off everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't think about how your body would look afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... if I eat something heavy, like the fried food from the break room, or if I get a big plate of food at one of the church potlucks, I just get so scared that it’s going to show on me right away. Like, instantly. I'll just keep thinking about it and obsessing, and I’ll want to go find a bathroom and check the scale right then and there. \n\nBut those church potlucks, honestly, they are a whole situation anyway. Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole, but she puts water chestnuts in it, which is just... it's a terrible texture. Who puts crunchy water chestnuts in a soft casserole? And Pastor Dave always eats like three helpings of it. I really don't know how he does it without spilling all over his ties. He wears the most ridiculous ties. He has this one with little neon golf clubs all over it, and another one that actually plays a song if you press a button hidden on the back. He wore the musical one to a Sunday service once, and I guess he leaned against the pulpit too hard because it just started blaring \"Jingle Bells\" right in the middle of his sermon. In April! It was so loud, and he couldn't figure out how to turn it off, so he just had to take the whole tie off and shove it in his pocket. He's always doing stuff like that. \n\nBut yeah, if I eat a big plate of that kind of food, I just panic about it changing my body and have to weigh myself immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, probably. Like, even if I didn't have a mirror, I'd still feel it, you know? I can feel how my clothes fit, or how my thighs touch when I walk. \n\nLike when I have to wear those heavy canvas work pants at the warehouse. They are the most uncomfortable pants in the world, I swear. The fabric is so stiff, it's like wearing cardboard, and the seams rub right against the inside of your knees. We have to wear them for safety because of the box cutters, but Luis—he's the one who broke the French press—he tried to get away with wearing these black denim jeans instead because they looked similar. But Brenda caught him because he bent over to pick up a roll of packing tape and the jeans ripped right down the middle seam. It was so loud, you could hear it over the conveyor belts. He had to tie his flannel shirt around his waist for the rest of his shift, and Brenda made him stand at the end of the line where everyone could see him. She's ruthless like that. She actually has this little notebook where she writes down every time someone messes up the dress code. \n\nBut yeah, even without a mirror, I’d still be checking how things fit and worrying about it. The feeling doesn't just go away because I can't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control over your weight if you started eating more?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know? I've never really thought about it as losing control, exactly. I'm not really sure what would happen. \n\nBut speaking of losing control, you should have seen line two at the warehouse yesterday. Total chaos. Someone—I think it was the new guy, Marcus—he loaded this massive shipment of ceramic garden gnomes onto the wrong belt. And the belt was going way too fast. Why do people even buy garden gnomes, anyway? They are so incredibly creepy. My aunt Sheila used to have this whole row of them leading up to her front porch, and over the years the paint chipped off their faces, so they just looked like these little zombie men staring at you when you walked up the steps. It used to freak me out so bad when I'd go over there after school. \n\nAnyway, Marcus's box of gnomes completely busted open at the end of the belt because it hit the stopper too hard, and there were just... shattered little red hats and ceramic beards everywhere. Luis was trying to scoop them all into a trash can with a flattened piece of cardboard like a makeshift dustpan, and Brenda was literally screaming over the loudspeaker. It took them like two hours to clear the jam. \n\nSo yeah, I guess I don't really know about the weight thing. I just haven't really tried eating more to find out what would happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel like you're losing control over your life?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it's about losing control over my whole life... I mean, I guess I haven't really thought about it like that. I'm not even sure what that would look like for me. \n\nBut honestly, my life feels pretty out of control sometimes anyway, especially with my schedule at the warehouse. They keep switching my shifts around without telling me until the day before. Last week, I was supposed to be on the morning shift, right? So I set my alarm for 4:30 AM, which is just brutal. My alarm clock is this ancient digital thing that my mom gave me, and the snooze button is broken, so you have to actually unplug it from the wall to make it stop beeping. It's the most annoying sound, like a dying smoke detector. Anyway, I dragged myself out of bed, unplugged the stupid clock, drove all the way there in the dark, and when I swiped my badge, the machine flashed red. Turns out Brenda had moved me to the swing shift and just... forgot to put it in the system. So I had to drive all the way back home and try to go back to sleep, but by then the neighbor's dog was outside barking at the mail carrier. That dog barks at literally everything. A leaf blows across the street? Barking. A car drives by? Barking. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know if eating more would make me feel like I'm losing control of my life, because I'm not really sure I have that much control over it to begin with.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that eating a normal amount of food would make you feel like you're not in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I feel like... didn't you just ask me something like that? I honestly don't have a good answer for you. I haven't tried eating a \"normal amount,\" whatever that even means, so I really don't know if it would make me feel out of control or not. \n\nBut speaking of normal food, you should see the stuff people eat in our breakroom at the warehouse. It is so disgusting. We only have this one microwave for like, fifty people on the floor, and the timer dial is literally held on with duct tape. On Tuesday, Luis actually brought in leftover tilapia. Fish! In a warehouse breakroom with zero windows. It smelled so horrendous that Marcus started gagging over by the water cooler, and Brenda had to prop the double doors open with these heavy wooden pallets just to air the room out. \n\nBut she left the pallets sticking out into the main forklift lane, so Dave—he's the safety manager—he came down and completely lost his mind. Dave has this really high-pitched voice when he gets mad, almost like a squeaky toy? He was yelling at Brenda and waving his clipboard around, and the clip snapped, so all his safety checklists went flying all over the concrete floor. It took three of us to help him pick them up, and the papers got all covered in that black warehouse dust. He was so mad he made Luis take his fish out to the dumpster in the parking lot. \n\nSo yeah, I really don't know the answer to your question. I just haven't tried it to see what would happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But Brenda caught him because he bent over to pick up a roll of packing tape and the jeans ripped right down the middle seam. It was so loud, you could hear it over the conveyor belts. He had to tie his flannel shirt around his waist for the rest of his shift, and Brenda made him stand at the end of the line where everyone could see him. She's ruthless like that. I mean, I don't really know how to answer that either. If I couldn't see my body? Like, if I was blind or something? I guess I haven't ever really thought about that. I'm not sure if I'd want to restrict or not. 

But talking about not being able to see things... the lighting in the warehouse is just terrible. The main vending machine has been out of order for three weeks, and Dave refuses to call the repair guy because he says we're wasting company time looking at snacks. It's this little terrier mix, and it just stands at the fence and yaps at literally nothing. I tried talking to the neighbor about it, but he just brushed me off. He’s always outside working on this old Chevy truck that he’s had on blocks in his driveway since I moved in.
